ParlaMint-AT_1996-02-28-020-XX-NRSITZ-00008_d7e12573	Mr President. High house! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! There's no question at all: Austria's energy policy is facing new challenges, and it is about ensuring energy supply at competitive prices, taking environmental aspects into account in a completely new competitive environment. However, I should like to make it clear here that I believe that energy policy must be more than prevention policy. It would be a trick to believe that the construction of power plants can be prevented without, on the other hand, increasing the dependence on import current. I think it is difficult to classify whether the imported electricity comes from nuclear power stations or other sources of power. I am in favour of exploiting all energy saving potentials. I agree that renewable energy sources are being forced. And I am in favour of doing so in the light of the criterion of competitiveness on the part of energy suppliers and the cost-effectiveness of energy users. – Of course, it is more often so that the aspects of economic and economic optimization are not identical. We must certainly think about how to improve coordination in this area. On the individual questions in detail. Questions 1 to 3: We are faced with the fact that Austrian electricity supply carriers are a number of companies whose organisational structure has been growing historically over the decades. Another fact is that these companies are privately organised and have to proceed according to commercial principles. In addition, from my point of view, more competition in the electricity sector is desirable. However, it is not to be denied that in recent times certain fractions have occurred between the interlocutory society and the national societies. [[Abg. Wabl: But this is very nice!]] Mr Wabl! But I wouldn't talk about a chaos. The interaction of Austrian electricity industry carriers, which has worked well for many years and is still present in many areas today, with the new situation in the European electricity markets – that is to mention examples, for example, the revolution that has caused the new gas turbine technologies, but also an oversupply of cheap electricity imports – must be developed organically. However, ladies and gentlemen, let us take care of central planning ideas and ideologies that want to shift responsibility from the companies to the state bureaucracies. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This path of energy policy has led our eastern neighbours to the dead end. However, I will try, regardless of how the debate on the internal energy market is going on in the EU, to create framework conditions that take into account the increased competitiveness of the Austrian electricity industry and to link more competitive elements in this context with supervisory, control and coordination mechanisms of the Ministry of Economic Affairs. I believe that more competition and increased regulatory supervisory mechanisms are quite compatible, the new system of energy supervision and electricity price monitoring. It is important to further develop the classical energy monitoring and control mechanisms and, in particular, to adapt them to the EU competition rules, e.g. in addition to electricity price control instruments such as concession for supply areas and the system authorisation procedure for electricity generation. We can certainly discuss whether the procedure is not only carried out at national level, but also at federal level. Other such instruments include the granting of authorisations for the import and export of electricity, the obligation to provide information for ETUUs and the possibility of viewing and/or the control and approval of general conditions of supply. Overall, the public obligations of the electricity sector must be guaranteed, which are summarised in the draft EU Internal Electricity Market Directive under the term "public service bonds". I hope to have answered your questions one to three with these introductory remarks. On the question 11: I'm not a determination of the 2nd. Nationalization law, which would allow me to intervene in the power plant expansion of the electricity industry in a coordinated manner. The so-called 2. Since November 1987, national law has been only the organizational statute of the Austrian electricity industry, which in particular regulates the division of tasks between the interconnected society, state companies and the five major state capital electricity supply companies and is based on the federalist structure of Austria. Within this framework, coordination instruments have developed very well between Austrian electricity companies, such as the coordinated development programme, as well as coordination agreements and the research programme of the electricity industry. On the other hand, the provisions of this Act cannot be taken into account by the Federal Government or by the Ministry of Economic Affairs. Also the energy and constitutional and administrative literature of the 2. Nationalization law and the jurisdiction of the Supreme Courts do not interpret in the law any steering and regulatory powers of a central federal body. If you want the latter or if you want to, then you have to make changes to the current legislation. From § 4 para. 1 of the 2nd. Nationalization law must be followed by the fact that large power plants within the meaning of this Act are power plants which are not intended to fulfil the task of the Landesgesellschaften and not as self-sufficiency plants under § 1 para. 2 lit. b falls. According to § 4 para. The Federal Government, after consulting the combined company, decides, taking into account energy and hydroeconomic concerns, whether a power plant should be regarded as a large-scale power plant in the above-mentioned sense. With regard to the question of when it comes to a procedure in accordance with § 4 para. 5 comes with the following: § 3 para. ==References== Nationalization law assigns the task of implementing the general supply of electricity in the field of the individual Länder to the state companies. This task includes, in particular, the production of electrical energy. According to § 5 para. 4 lit. d 2. Nationalization law is the task of the Association Society to arrange the construction and operation of large power plants by existing or to be set up special companies. In the interpretation of § 4 para. ==References== It must therefore be assumed that a declaration to a large-scale power plant must be made only in cases where the construction of a power plant by a special company is or should be caused by the combined company. As this is not the case with the Lambach power plant project, the conditions for the initiation of a procedure in accordance with § 4 para. 5 2. Nationalization law on large-scale power plant declaration not provided. A consultation of the joint venture is therefore unnecessary. Furthermore, it is not surprising that the joint venture did not claim the Lambbach project for a special company. Question 15: I would like to begin by referring to my introductory remarks, in which I outlined a programme to strengthen the supervisory and coordination mechanisms in the field of electricity management. However, once again I warn of overly energy planning thoughts and I refer to the given constitutional law situation. In accordance with the federalist structure of the Austrian electricity supply, Article 12 of the Federal Constitutional Act only assigns competence to the federal government for the principle legislation, while the implementing legislation and enforcement constitute a matter for the countries. As a result of this constitutional situation of competence, I am not able to intervene in the field of power plant planning. Nevertheless, I would also like to say that coordination of power plant planning is primarily a task to be carried out by the undertakings concerned in accordance with the competition rules. Nor can it be the task of coordinated power plant planning to compensate for any misevaluations of individual undertakings in future energy sales at the expense of other companies. In any case, I reject the direct ad hoc intervention of central bodies. In addition, I believe that the Austrian electricity supply companies are performing their coordination function responsibly, which is particularly evident in the coordinated development programme of the combined society, the state companies and the national capital companies. A coordinated expansion plan does not mean that overcapacity or even low supply capacities can be avoided in all cases, especially since investment decisions or decisions on long-term supply contracts usually take place at a time when this cannot be accurately estimated and can always be clearly defined. On the question 16: The study Least-Cost-Planning mentioned in the question has not yet been adopted by the Federal Ministers for the Mission. The question of any implementation of the LCP instrument, an extended approach to Integrated Resource Planning and measures in this direction can, however, be discussed and discussed after completion of the studies. I believe that these instruments must be integrated into the electricity-efficient authorisation procedure of electricity generation plants and that it should be clarified at what level this should be done. On the question 17: I would like to point out that the new electricity price monitoring system of the Federal Minister for Economic Affairs does not detach from the fixing of electricity prices, nor does it allow electricity suppliers to grant fixed monopoly rates. I have to vote against the idea that I would like to put down the "disgusting" electricity price scheme. Rather, I am simplifying the current electricity price system by means of a modern and flexible supervisory and information system and by removing, as I believe, a government-owned bureaucratic steering ideology. But if it is necessary and necessary, I will not be afraid to initiate and conclude current pricing procedures. As far as your view is concerned that the new system guarantees pensions, I would ask you to study the detailed documents I submitted on the occasion of the introduction of the new system to the public. It is clear that a comprehensive electricity supply in a given area contains those elements which are described in economic theory as natural monopoly. But it is here that the new system brings more transparency, which the old formalized system did not have. A pension could at most be referred to if the permissible price were to be based on the consumer price alone. But this is not the case, there is not only the stringent price cap according to Anglo-American model, but also a productivity reduction. Moreover, the level of both fees and the procedure at all have been thoroughly clarified and confirmed by renowned university experts. On the question 18: The collective contracts of the electricity supply companies workers/employees, labour regulations and various operating agreements with regard to allowances and special services fall within the collective autonomy of the respective electricity supply companies. Already in the past, at the initiative of the Minister of Economic Affairs, the electricity supply companies were stopped to take effective measures in particular towards reducing personnel costs – keyword: quantitative savings targets, pension reforms et cetera. These are now also recognised by the Court of Auditors, for example in the recent report on the results of the joint venture's audit. However, due to the strengthening of competition, particularly in the context of the proposed internal electricity market and further privatisation, it is clear that there is a significant rationalisation pressure which will have to and will lead to a further reduction in staff costs and also to more stringent personnel structures. On question 19: The fact is that the programme of support referred to in the 1993 energy report under measure M 52 is already being implemented. In particular, I would like to refer to wind power plants supported by the Eco Fund in 1955 with an electric power of more than 11 MW, for which more than 48 million schilling have already been promised. In addition, under the title "Wind Force for Austria" under the EU-Altener Funding Programme, the project, which was carried out by the Environmental Advisory Board of Austria, was funded by the public authorities. In addition, the energy section of my house has set up a working group which is deeply concerned with tariff measures to force renewable energy sources, in particular wind energy. Question 20: With regard to the general agreement agreed with the electricity industry in February 1994 on the basis of a National Council resolution on the promotion of electricity consumption from photovoltaic, wind and biomass plants, I think it is appropriate to re-evaluate the subsidies provided for in the Agreement and to allow them to be incorporated into the intended wind energy programme. On question 21: In the sense of federalism, the setting of minimum prices for electricity supplies which do not exceed the national borders is to be determined by the authority familiar with local events. A decision on this feed-in situation at national level therefore ensures the best possible reflection of regional cost structures. On the question 22: The question as to the extent to which the network provision fee referred to by you within the meaning of § 4 of the NÖ-EEC is appropriate does not fall within my jurisdiction, but is a matter assigned to the Lower Austrian Government for supply. On the question 23: At the present stage, I will inform you that the Association Society has developed the general project of a 380 kV line between the existing Kainachtal substation in the western Styrian municipality of Schwarzing and the planned substation of Südburgenland in the municipality of Rotenturm an der Pinka. In the course of this line a substation is also to be built in the East Styria. For this 380 kV management project, my department carries out the so-called pre-examination procedure according to the Starkstromwegengesetz. In the course of this process, an international opinion of university professors Edwin, Aachen and Glavitsch, Zurich, was prepared on the energy-efficient necessity of this management. I have sent this opinion to the interlocutory company for the preservation of its party hearing. All further procedural steps depend on the reaction of the joint society. The question which section of this management, which will cover its whole range from Kainachtal to Vienna, will first build the interlocutory company, must be answered in the company's own business decision-making area. Apart from this, the sub-section between South Burgenland and Vienna is to be referred to as a priority from an energy-economic point of view, because there are currently only very old and not particularly powerful 110 kV lines in the Burgenland. Through these weak 110 kV lines, the Burgenland, which has no significant own electricity generation and is almost all the electricity required from the interconnector, can no longer be supplied sufficiently and safely, I say. Question 24: It is not my job to comment on demented press reports. However, I do not want to inform you of my fundamental considerations on climate change and energy policy. The reduction of greenhouse gas emissions is a task that goes beyond the energy sector without prejudice to a high-energy component. At the federal level, it falls within the competence of the Federal Minister for the Environment, who has drawn up the Austrian Climate Change Report in accordance with a resolution of the House. There are two key measures in the energy sector: the saving of energy and the replacement of fossil energy sources by renewables. This is in the general direction of Austrian energy policy, whose objective, in addition to the security and cost-effectiveness of supply, is environmental and social compatibility as well as the use of domestic resources. Accordingly, the 97 measures of the 1993 energy concept comprise the areas mentioned above. These measures, which are equally important to the environment and energy policy, also include the expansion of hydropower. Austria is seeking an exemplary role in the Community of industrialised countries, but must also accept the limits of action within the international framework. As an example, I would like to mention the increased tank tourism, which, after increasing the tax on mineral oil, has dampened both the environmental and financial effects. However, I believe that further action in the field of energy taxation, which the new Federal Government is planning, will also mean further steps in the right direction. I agree with you on the need to force renewable energy. However, it appears that its concern is to provide, in addition to certain renewable primary energy forms, binding on the energy industry. It seems to me that the choice between renewable primary energy forms is a decision which, on the one hand, should largely be assigned to the entrepreneurial discretion of the supply companies, on the other hand, in the sense of cooperative federalism, to the regulatory area of the countries. Question 25: This too seems to me to be important: the OECD study, quoted by Mr Anschober. I would warn against shortening energy policy discussions on the consideration of less or even a single parameter. As a significant example, I would like to point out that, in particular, the use of combined heat and power in Denmark since the end of the 1970s has been based on a very high use of coal, which has resulted in very high CO2 emissions. In the environmentally significant measure of CO2 emissions per capita and per year, Denmark is 11,4 tonnes much worse than Austria, with 7,1 tonnes of each comparison in the group of industrialised countries. As a major producer of gas, Denmark currently has other possibilities to go in these CO2-favourable but, of course, non-CO2-neutral energy sources than Austria, for example, is undisputed, but also shows a bit of questionability of the comparison. However, if you have already focused on primary energy consumption, I would like to point out that in the period 1980 to 1994 energy consumption in Denmark increased by 2.2%, in Austria by 8.6%. Taking into account the overall economic development, relative energy consumption – energy consumption per unit of gross domestic product – which, I believe, gives the better picture – is the following decline: Denmark minus 22.9 percent, Austria minus 19.1 percent. The difference in improving energy efficiency is therefore by no means as high as you have described it here. In the EU area, Austria is behind Luxembourg, Ireland and Denmark in fourth position. Considering a longer period since the first oil price shock in 1973, according to current data from the international energy agency, there is even a parallel development of both countries. Both Denmark and Austria needed around 0.15 oil units of total energy per 1000 units of gross domestic product in 1994. Between 1973 and 1994, both countries achieved a fall in relative energy consumption by around 29 percent. In the case of per capita consumption, Austria is even significantly better off with 3.3 tonnes of oil units than Denmark with 4,02 tonnes of oil units. The slightly better data from Denmark for the period 1980 to 1994 is mainly due to the low share of industry in energy consumption – Denmark has 20 percent, Austria has 30 percent – as well as the above mentioned expansion of district heating supply mainly on a coal-based basis. I thank you for the attention.

Therefore, I've been more intensive about what it really looks like with the electricity requirement: What does the Energy Report say? What do the experts say? – There is a very young study by Wifo, written by university professors Dr. Jansen and Dr. Musil under the title "The application and need of electrical energy in Austria until 2005. In this I have found the supposed discrepancy, why they say that there is so much electricity produced anyway, that we cannot consume it at all, and the others say that we very much need a power plant. – There is currently no overcapacity, according to the authors of the study, under the proven assumption of reserve maintenance for a high-security power supply. This reserve is used to bridge economic fluctuations, underwaters, power plant failures. It is practised to a similar extent in most EU countries. Reduced reserve maintenance reduces security of supply, potentially endangering the economic location, but could very well be shifted to other nations, such as the Bayern-Werke et cetera. Mr Hofmann! Is he still there? - No, he's not here anymore. In fact, it has brought figures on electricity production and consumption in Upper Austria. However, the above reserve is discrepancy. I am politically convinced that we Austrians are very likely to maintain this reserve, for which we have also committed ourselves internationally. There is therefore no overcapacity, and we need this power plant very well. The trend in the energy report, despite the realization of savings potential, is expected to increase the demand for electricity. If you know how long you need to build a power plant, then it is unthinkable to start building a power plant that starts in 1996 from the electricity demand of 1994. You know very well that in terms of how electricity demand will develop, you have to plan ten years ahead and longer. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dr. Frischenschlager.]] [[Further calls to the Liberal Forum and the Greens.]] It is also stated in the energy report that the whole energy policy in Austria is being pursued by hydrothermal compounds, namely that there are two thirds of hydroelectric power plants and one third of calorie power plants. This also eliminates the argument of the "twining", which is repeatedly cited, as a false argument. Upper Austria is based on this requirement: two thirds of hydropower and one third of caloric power plants. The structure of the Austrian electricity generation and supply plants is characterized by a close cooperation between association and Landes-EVUs, even though – as mentioned several times here today – this cooperation does not always run smoothly. The defined division of tasks still works. That is to say, subsidiarity is taken into account by the federal structure, and the central structure of the Verbund AG coordinates and ensures transport and reserve maintenance. The production and sales function is mainly carried by the state companies. And for this task, namely production and sale, the OKA Lambach is building. This ensures customer proximity and efficiency, and this should remain the case. This system has proved to be excellent so far, and through even closer cooperation or mutual restraint – Mr Van der Bellen is not here now – one will be able to stand up for the future liberalisation of the European market. I'm not for sale. Mr Van der Bellen and Mr Kier have done this. You just smoked a cigarette, I hope you enjoy it. I am not for the sale of the Austrian e-business to Bavaria or to the French. I do not want Austria to become an enclave of the atomic lobby. Austrian interests must be safeguarded! Nationalization law must be replaced by a new regulation, creating a new energy organisation law that will bring more market, but which, according to my political understanding, must also bring decentralisation. Austria is pursuing a modified single-buyer model in its energy liberalisation. As a big customer, I welcome this. That means: It is not only allowed to import and export the association, it is also allowed to do so by the Landesgesellschaften. ParlaMint-AT_1996-02-28-020-XX-NRSITZ-00008_d7e15092	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! [[Congratulations with representatives of the SPÖ.]] This may well be justified from the business strategic and operational point of view of the OKA, however, whether this power plant construction is useful in the light of the Austrian energy policy as a whole, but it really needs to be considered more precisely. Sure: The Kraftwerk Lambach is included in the coordinated expansion programme. The coordinated development programme is an instrument in which the various energy supply companies agree on planned capacity expansion and coordinated operation. This coordination is necessary with a view to the three-part construction of the Austrian electricity industry – a composite society, state companies and municipal companies – because competition against each other instead of a common procedure under the competitive conditions of the European internal market would lead to a disputing Austrian energy industry becoming largely foreign in the medium term. – On this point I share the opinion of Mr Kier. The Austrian energy industry combined is really a dwarf compared to leading European electricity companies such as the Preussag, RWE or the Electricité de France. We should always be aware of this in all the discussions we are having here, especially in the context of the discussions on the Lambach power plant, which we are discussing today. All the more necessary is a new Energy Organisation Act, as already provided for in the last Government Convention, but no longer as a result of the dissolution of the National Council. There is a need to create framework conditions that will allow the electricity industry to react internally to the increasing competitive pressure and to prepare organisationally for the expected Internal Electricity Market Directive. An analysis of the Austrian electricity market shows that electricity consumption in the field of public electricity supply is stagnating or is even partly declining. The stagnation of electricity consumption among the Landesgesellschaft and thus also with the combined company results mainly from the increasing self-sufficiency of large customers as well as the increase in the cost-effective share of self-sufficiency due to combined heat and power in the municipalities. As this trend is expected to continue, both Austrian market development and the need to adapt the organisational structure of the electricity industry to the changed market conditions are both due to the European Union's competitive approach. Required is a concept that enables the public order to be secured and high-quality power supply to be combined with an orderly competition. The Commission's internal market approach aims to enable large customers from 100 gigawatt hours of annual electricity loss and distributors to make direct contact with the supplier of their choice, leaving the individual States the way in which they implement the competitive approach. The public debate gives the impression that it would already be possible to adapt to the challenges ahead by further privatisation of the e-commerce and that the market would regulate everything. Ladies and gentlemen! A scale comparison with European competitors EdF, RWE, ENEL, Bayernwerk, Preussag et cetera, each of which is actually larger than all Austrian EVUs combined, shows that they could buy and subordinate to any Austrian electrical processing company for sale. Ladies and gentlemen! I believe it is important that in the future we succeed in considering the provision of low-cost energy as an important part of the economic infrastructure, to realize that we have to stand up for security of supply and prevent disturbances and, above all, to ensure that Austria's economic location is safeguarded by a targeted energy policy. Austrian environmental policy – for example, the achievement of the Toronto target – must not be undermined by eco-dumping, for example by importing cheap electricity from plants that would be banned in Austria – I think in this case nuclear power plants. This applies to both domestic and foreign energy providers. I would have made a number of other proposals, but because of the advanced period, I would just like to sum up more that energy policy has been much more than a power plant building programme for some time. Optimizations and efficiency improvements have long been taking place. There has long been cooperation with industry and households to minimise energy consumption – and as another important task of international networking. With EU accession, issues of the competitiveness of our infrastructure have also become virulent. Who, in the face of these problems, is only referring to a discussion of power plant construction: yes or no? Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report. - Thank you very much.

Furthermore, other regulations, such as the Live Animals Regulations created by the IATA, the International Air Transport Association, are not binding legislation. It is therefore certainly necessary to make these legal provisions now because, in the event of infringement, no sanctions would have been possible. Finally, there is also an EU directive which we essentially adopt with this animal transport law. I believe that with this animal transport law air we create extremely sensible and very animal-friendly legislation. For example, transport capacity is restricted, it is also required that experienced, trained companions monitor transport, that a path as gentle as possible be chosen, and that transport containers be adapted to, inter alia, the needs of the respective animals. Mrs Jäger has taken over the report. It will initiate the debate.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-02-28-020-XX-NRSITZ-00008_d7e16620	Mr President. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! I shall bring the report of the Committee on Foreign Affairs on the Government template (8 of the Supplements): Agreement between the Republic of Austria and the Republic of Slovenia on the use of two parts of the Slovenian state area in the area of the ski area "Threelandereck". The present agreement aims to establish a cross-border ski resort on Austrian and Slovenian territory, as well as the necessary infrastructure, as well as permission to use this ski area by Austrian and Slovenian citizens as well as third-country nationals who are not subject to the obligation of sight in the Republic of Austria or in the Republic of Slovenia. At its meeting on 31 December, the Foreign Policy Committee held the present State Treaty. Negotiating in January 1996. At the time of the vote, it was unanimously decided to recommend to the National Council the approval of the conclusion of this State Treaty. In the present case, the Foreign Policy Committee shall keep the adoption of a special federal law in accordance with Art. Paragraph 50 2 B-VG for the fulfilment of the State Treaty. As a result of its deliberations, the Committee on Foreign Affairs therefore proposes that the National Council: The conclusion of the State Treaty: Agreement between the Republic of Austria and the Republic of Slovenia on the use of two parts of the Slovenian state area in the area of the ski area "Three Country corner" (8 of the supplements) is hereby approved. Mr President. In the event that word reports are available, I would like to go into the debate.
